I just don't know anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up in the morning?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it’s been about a month. Yes, just about a month now, ever since my sister died. It was so unexpected, you know. One minute she was fine and the next... well. I've spent the last few weeks trying to sort through her apartment, and oh, the amount of yarn she had! She was a knitter. Sweaters, scarves, little booties for babies that didn't even exist yet. I told her once, I said, Margaret, you're going to drown in all this merino wool, but she just laughed and bought another skein of this awful mustard yellow color. I don't know who looks good in mustard yellow, certainly neither of us with our complexion, we wash out so easily... but anyway, yes, I suppose it was right around then, about a month ago when she passed, that these thoughts started creeping in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as gardening, as much as you did before your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. The gardening... it just feels like work now. It doesn't give me that little lift it used to. And it's the same with cooking for the neighbors. I've always been the one to bring over a dish if someone's under the weather. I make a lovely baked ziti, though I have to be careful with Mr. Henderson next door because he absolutely cannot have dairy, it upsets his stomach terribly, so I usually do a roasted lemon chicken for him with fresh rosemary from the yard. Margaret used to say my rosemary bush was going to swallow the house whole, it's just massive, you really have to prune it aggressively in the fall... but anyway, even the cooking just feels empty. \n\nI still make myself show up for things, of course. I go to my Tuesday book club, and the Thursday dance night at the community center. We're reading a mystery novel right now about a baker, and honestly, the plot is just ridiculous. The detective completely ignores the flour bins in the very first chapter, and anyone who has ever baked knows that's exactly where you'd hide something! It just makes no sense. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, lately, when I go to these things, the book club or the dances, I just sit there feeling like I'm behind a pane of glass. I'm in the room, and I'm smiling, but I can't quite connect to any of the joy of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts of not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, guilty... I don't know if guilt is the right word for the mornings, but I do feel this awful, heavy guilt about Margaret. I keep going over and over those last few weeks before she passed, just torturing myself, really. I keep telling myself I should have called her more often, or listened better when she complained about being tired. I should have done something more useful. Even though part of me knows, logically, I couldn't have changed what happened. It was a heart attack, they said. Very sudden. \n\nBut you know, my cousin Arthur had a heart attack back in '98, and he survived it just fine. He completely changed his diet, gave up red meat entirely, which was a tragedy because he made the most incredible brisket for Passover. Oh, the secret was he used a whole bottle of ginger ale in the braising liquid, sounds strange but it tenderized the meat beautifully. He lived another twenty years on chicken and fish. But Margaret... she loved her sweets. She'd always have a little tin of those Danish butter cookies on her coffee table. The ones that come in the blue tin? I always ended up using the empty tins for my sewing supplies. I must have six of those blue tins stacked in my closet right now, full of buttons and thread... but yes, the guilt. I just can't shake the feeling that I should have been better to her at the end.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts of not wanting to wake up been present every day since they started?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... every day? I suppose it's hard to keep track, really. The days just sort of blur together lately. But yes, I think it's been pretty much every morning. I'll open my eyes and just look at the ceiling, and the ceiling in my bedroom has this terrible popcorn texture. They put it in back in the seventies, I think. My husband, rest his soul, he always said he was going to scrape it off and paint it a nice, smooth eggshell white. He bought all the tools—the scrapers, the drop cloths, even a special mask so he wouldn't breathe in the dust. But then he got busy with work, he was an accountant, you know, and tax season was always just a nightmare for him. He'd come home with his eyes completely bloodshot from staring at those little numbers all day. So the ceiling never got done. I still have the scrapers in the garage, actually, sitting right next to the old lawnmower that hasn't worked since 2015. I really should call someone to haul that away, it's just taking up space... but yes, what were we talking about? Oh, the mornings. Yes, I'd say it's been every day. Just this heavy feeling right when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, I've been trying to think if there's anything... but honestly, no. Like I mentioned before, I still make myself show up for things, you know? I go to the book club, I go to the Thursday dance night. But it's just... I sit there and it feels like I'm behind a pane of glass. I'm there, but I'm not really *there*. \n\nAnd it's such a shame because the dance night used to be so lively. They have this instructor, Hector, he's from Argentina, I think. Or maybe Chile? Somewhere in South America. He has the most wonderful rhythm, and he always wears these very shiny shoes. I asked him once how he keeps them so scuff-free, and he said it's all about the polish he orders special from overseas. It comes in a little glass jar with a gold lid. I tried to find it online once for my nephew's graduation present—he was starting a job at a bank and needed to look sharp—but the website was all in Spanish, and I couldn't figure out the shipping. My nephew ended up getting a job at a tech company anyway, where they just wear those hooded sweatshirts all day. I don't understand how you can feel professional in a sweatshirt, but he says they pay him very well, so who am I to judge? \n\nBut yes... pleasure. No, not really. Even my garden, and cooking for the neighbors... it just doesn't give me that little lift it used to. It's all just going through the motions now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't stepped on a scale recently, but I suppose my clothes are fitting a bit looser these days. It's just that food doesn't really call to me much anymore. I'll make myself a little piece of toast in the morning, maybe some soup, and then suddenly it's evening and I realize I've barely eaten a thing all day. And I'm certainly not trying to lose weight or anything like that. \n\nI used to love making soup, though. My mother had this wonderful recipe for chicken noodle that she brought over from the old country. She always said the secret was to roast the chicken bones first before you make the broth, it gives it this deep, golden color. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she'd use, it weighed an absolute ton. When she passed, my sister Margaret—bless her—she insisted on taking the pot, even though she barely cooked. She just wanted it for the sentimental value, I suppose. I remember helping her carry it out to her car, it was a blue Buick LeSabre, one of those big boat-like cars they used to make. The trunk on that thing was massive, you could fit a whole week's worth of groceries and still have room for three suitcases. We took that car on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once, back in... oh, it must have been 1985. The air conditioning broke down somewhere in South Dakota, and we had to drive with the windows down the whole way. My hair was an absolute disaster by the time we got to the hotel. But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the weight. Yes, I suppose I have lost a bit of weight just from not having much of an appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? I'm not really sure I've noticed anything like that. I mean, I suppose I don't rush around the way I did when I was younger, but that's just getting older, isn't it? I remember when I used to be able to run up and down the stairs with a full basket of laundry. The laundry room was in the basement of our first house, and the stairs were so steep. They were covered in this awful green shag carpet that was impossible to vacuum properly. I had this Hoover upright, the kind with the bag you had to empty out, and it always smelled faintly of dust and burnt rubber. I took it in to get fixed once at this little shop on Main Street. The man who ran it, Mr. Henderson, he wore these thick glasses and always had a pencil tucked behind his ear. He told me the belt was going, which is why it smelled like that. He had all these old radios in his shop too, the big wooden ones from the 1930s. He said he fixed them up as a hobby. I always thought that was a nice hobby, bringing something old back to life. My husband's hobby was golf, which just meant he was gone every Saturday morning from April to October. He had this set of clubs he bought at a garage sale for fifty dollars, and he was so proud of them. He'd spend hours polishing the irons in the garage... but no, I haven't really noticed myself moving any slower than usual. Just the normal pace for someone my age, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're reacting more slowly to things than you used to?\n\nPatient: Reacting more slowly? Hmm... well, like I was just saying about the moving, I don't really think so. My reflexes seem perfectly fine to me. Just last week I actually managed to catch a glass right before it rolled off the kitchen counter. It was one of those tall iced tea glasses with the little yellow lemons painted on the side. \n\nI bought a whole set of them at a yard sale years ago. The lady selling them said they belonged to her aunt who used to live down in Florida. Florida... I've only been down there once, you know. We went to Orlando, but it rained almost the entire week we were there. It was that heavy, warm rain that just never seems to stop. We ended up spending three days just playing gin rummy in the hotel lobby. The carpet in that lobby had this awful geometric pattern, all bright oranges and browns, very 1970s. It honestly made you a bit dizzy if you stared at it too long. My husband kept joking that the ugly carpet was the real tourist attraction. He had such a dry sense of humor. He could keep a completely straight face while saying the absolute most ridiculous things to the hotel staff. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my reactions. No, I don't feel like I'm any slower to react to things. I caught that glass before it shattered all over the linoleum, so I suppose I'm still doing alright in that department.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or pleasure, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Joy? Well... I don't know about joy, really. I mean, I still make myself show up for things, you know? Like my Tuesday book club or the Thursday dance night at the community center. I go, but lately I just sit there feeling like I'm... I don't know, like I'm behind a pane of glass watching everyone else. \n\nThe community center used to be an old elementary school, actually. They kept the original hardwood floors in the gymnasium where we have the dances. They're that beautiful, narrow-plank maple, polished to a high shine. You don't see floors like that anymore. My grandfather was a carpenter, and he always said that maple was the only wood you should use for a floor if you wanted it to outlast you. He built this incredible roll-top desk for my grandmother when they got married. It had all these tiny little cubbyholes and secret drawers. I used to hide my marbles in there when I was a little girl. I had this one marble, a shooter, that was clear glass with a swirl of deep cobalt blue right through the middle. I won it off Tommy Miller in the third grade. Tommy was always getting into trouble for bringing frogs into the classroom. He'd catch them in the creek behind his house and keep them in his pockets. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, you asked about things that bring me joy. Even the things I used to love doing at home... like my garden, or cooking meals for the neighbors when someone was sick... they just don't give me that little lift they used to. I go through the motions, but the feeling just isn't there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are slower than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Slower thoughts? Well, I suppose... yes, I think they might be. I've noticed it when I try to sit down with the household accounts. I'll stare at the same budgeting spreadsheet, or I'll try to read a paragraph for this local history workshop I signed up for, and I have to read it three or four times. I just don't take in what it says. \n\nThe workshop is about the founding families of our county, actually. It's fascinating how much history is just sitting right under our noses. Did you know the old grist mill on Route 9 was built by a family who came over on a boat from Scotland in the 1700s? They brought the original millstones all the way across the Atlantic. Imagine the weight of those stones in the hold of a wooden ship! My great-uncle used to work in a shipyard in Boston, building those massive cargo vessels during the war. He was a riveter. He'd come home with his hands completely black from the grease and metal dust. His wife, my Aunt Clara, she used to make this special soap out of lye and bacon fat just to get his hands clean. It smelled terrible, like rancid meat and chemicals, but it worked. Clara was always making things from scratch. She used to can her own peaches, too. She had this huge copper kettle she'd boil the jars in... \n\nBut, yes, I suppose my mind just doesn't grab onto things the way it used to. I just end up staring at the page and nothing sinks in.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat interested in the things you're doing, even if it was just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Interested? Well, it's really just like I was mentioning a few minutes ago. I haven't had a single day this whole month where I felt truly engaged. I still make myself go to the book club and the dances, but that feeling of being stuck behind a pane of glass hasn't lifted for even an afternoon. \n\nI tried working in the garden, too, hoping that would help. I went out to prune the hydrangeas a few weeks ago. They're the 'Endless Summer' variety, you know. They bloom on both old and new wood, which is just wonderful if you live in a place that gets those unexpected late spring frosts. I bought my first bush at this little nursery that used to be out on County Road 4. It was run by this sweet couple, the Macys. Mrs. Macy always wore these enormous straw hats with big silk sunflowers pinned to the brim. She said the sun gave her terrible freckles, though I always thought freckles were quite charming. My daughter had a little bridge of freckles right across her nose when she was seven. She absolutely despised them. She actually tried to scrub them off with lemon juice and baking soda because she read about it in one of those magazines her older cousin left at our house. It just made her skin terribly red and angry-looking for a week... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, feeling interested. Right. Well, no. Even when I was out there with the hydrangeas, or when I made that casserole for the neighbors last week... I just go through the motions. I don't get that little lift from any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in your energy levels since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Energy levels? Oh, well... yes, since she passed, everything just feels so heavy. Even simple errands or getting the papers together for the Cultural Center leave me feeling completely worn out. It's like my body is moving through thick mud, you know? \n\nI was supposed to organize the archives for the Cultural Center this month. They have all these wonderful old photographs from when the town was first settled. There's this one picture of the main street from 1912, and it's just dirt roads and horses. The general store in the picture was owned by a man named Elias Thorne. He actually brought the first automobile into town, a Model T Ford. People used to line up just to watch him start the engine. My grandfather told me about it. He said Elias was always trying out new gadgets. He even ordered one of those early washing machines from a Sears Roebuck catalog. It had a hand crank and a wooden tub. Before that, my grandmother used a washboard and a tub of boiling water. She had these enormous cast-iron pots she'd heat over an open fire in the yard. She'd use that same lye soap I mentioned earlier. It's a wonder her hands didn't just peel right off... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my energy. Yes, it's just... I just don't have it anymore. I get so tired just thinking about doing things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty concentrating on things, such as reading the newspaper or watching television, since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Concentrating... well, yes. It's really just like I was saying a few minutes ago about my budgeting spreadsheet and that history workshop. I just end up reading the same paragraph three or four times and nothing sticks. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the newspaper. I try to sit down with the Sunday edition, mostly because my sister and I always used to do the crossword puzzle together. She was so remarkably good at them. She always used a fountain pen, never a pencil. She said using a pencil meant you lacked conviction. She had this beautiful silver Parker pen that she bought when we took a trip to London back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We stayed in this tiny little hotel near Russell Square. The wallpaper in our room was absolutely atrocious, big yellow cabbage roses everywhere. And the radiator clanked all night long. We ended up having to stuff a rolled-up towel under it just to get some peace. The concierge was terribly apologetic, though. A lovely young man named Arthur. He gave us extra scones at breakfast to make up for it. They had the most wonderful clotted cream there, so thick you could practically stand a spoon up in it... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so sorry. But yes, my concentration is just dreadful lately. Whether it's the paper or the accounts, I just stare at the page and my mind goes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm or suicide besides the thoughts of not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Self-harm? Oh, no... no, I don't have any sort of plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I would never do that. But... well, it's just like I was saying, what really scared me enough to finally come in and ask for help is that I keep having these mornings where I just lie there and think it might be so much easier if I just didn't wake up at all. \n\nIt's usually right around 6:00 AM when I wake up. My sister was always an early riser, too. She used to say that the best part of the day was before the rest of the world had a chance to ruin it. She had this little routine where she'd make a pot of Earl Grey tea—loose leaf, never bags—and sit on her back porch watching the birds. She had three different bird feeders out there. One was specifically for hummingbirds, and she'd boil her own sugar water for it. She was always so particular about the ratio, four parts water to one part sugar. She said if you made it too sweet, it was bad for their little livers. Or was it their kidneys? I can't quite remember. But she'd sit out there in this old wicker rocking chair that used to belong to our great-aunt Martha. Martha brought that chair all the way from Pennsylvania when she moved here in the forties. It had this awful faded floral cushion on it that smelled faintly of mothballs, no matter how many times we washed it...\n\nOh, goodness, I'm so sorry. I've completely lost the thread again. What were you asking? Oh, right, about hurting myself. No, no plans to do anything like that. Just... just those mornings where I think it would be easier not to wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking more slowly? I... I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. I suppose I might be, but no one has mentioned it to me. \n\nMy sister, though, she always spoke so quickly. Especially when she was excited about something. She could talk a mile a minute. I remember when we were little girls, she used to try and recite the entire poem 'The Night Before Christmas' in one breath. She'd get so red in the face by the time she reached 'the moon on the breast of the new-fallen snow.' Our mother would always scold her and tell her she was going to make herself faint. Mother was very strict about elocution. She made us practice reading aloud from the newspaper every evening after dinner. We had to stand up straight with a book balanced on our heads. If the book fell, we had to start the whole article over again. It was usually a heavy encyclopedia, too. The 'M' volume, I think, because it was the thickest. We had this beautiful set of Encyclopedia Britannica bound in dark green leather. My father bought it from a traveling salesman who came to the door in a torrential downpour. His suit was completely soaked through, and I think my father just felt sorry for him. He bought the whole set on an installment plan... \n\nOh, dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I apologize. But no, I haven't noticed myself speaking any slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you're just going through the motions with the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, I suppose it's been a few months now. I still make myself show up for things, you know, like my book club or the Thursday dance night. But lately, I just sit there feeling like I'm behind a pane of glass. Even my garden and cooking for the neighbors don't give me that little lift they used to. \n\nI used to love baking for Mr. and Mrs. Higgins next door. Poor Mrs. Higgins broke her hip back in the spring. Slipped right on her front walkway on a patch of wet leaves. They had to put three pins in it. Her daughter came all the way up from Florida to help her recover, but honestly, the daughter was more of a hindrance than a help. She kept trying to rearrange Mrs. Higgins' kitchen cabinets while she was laid up in bed. Who does that? She moved all the good china to the bottom shelf where anyone could just bump into it. It was Royal Doulton, too, the Old Balloon Seller pattern, I believe. My grandmother had a few pieces of that same pattern, but she kept them in a beautiful locked mahogany curio cabinet in the formal dining room. The key was this tiny, delicate brass thing that she kept on a black velvet ribbon. She used to let me hold it when I was a very little girl, but I had to wash my hands with lye soap first so I wouldn't tarnish it...\n\nOh, goodness me, I've wandered completely off again, haven't I? I am so sorry. But yes, it's been a few months now that I've just felt disconnected from it all, just going through the motions without any of the joy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Better? No... no, I haven't really had any good days. It's just been this constant, heavy feeling for about a month now. Ever since she died so unexpectedly. 

It was a Tuesday when it happened. I always remember because Tuesday is the day the street sweepers come through our neighborhood. They make such a dreadful racket, don't they? You have to move your car off the street by 8:00 AM or they give you a fifty-dollar ticket. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, got three tickets in one month last year because he kept forgetting. He’s getting quite forgetful these days, poor man. He used to be a high school history teacher. Taught European history, I think. Or maybe it was American? No, it must have been European, because he always talked about a trip he took to Prague in the seventies. He brought back this beautiful bohemian glass vase. It was a deep ruby red with gold etching all around the rim. He kept it right in his front window where the afternoon sun would catch it. It cast the most beautiful red shadows across his living room floor. He had hardwood floors, you know, the original oak from when the houses were built in the twenties. They don't make floors like that anymore, do they? Everything is that cheap laminate now that bubbles up if you spill a drop of water...

Oh, dear, I've done it again. I'm so sorry, I just keep drifting off. What were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling better. No, it's just been a steady month of this awful fog since she passed.

My sister was always an early riser, too. She used to say that the best part of the day was before the rest of the world had a chance to ruin it. She had this little routine where she'd make a pot of Earl Grey tea—loose leaf, never bags—and sit on her back porch watching the birds. She had three different bird feeders out there. One was specifically for hummingbirds, and she'd boil her own sugar water for it. She was always so particular about the ratio, four parts water to one part sugar. She said if you made it too sweet, it was bad for their little livers. Or was it their kidneys? I can't quite remember. But she'd sit out there in this old wicker rocking chair that used to belong to our great-aunt Martha. Martha brought that chair all the way from Pennsylvania when she moved here in the forties. What were you asking? Oh, right, about hurting myself. No, no plans to do anything like that. Just... just those mornings where I think it would be easier not to wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking more slowly? I... I remember when we were little girls, she used to try and recite the entire poem 'The Night Before Christmas' in one breath. She'd get so red in the face by the time she reached 'the moon on the breast of the new-fallen snow.' Our mother would always scold her and tell her she was going to make herself faint. Mother was very strict about elocution. She made us practice reading aloud from the newspaper every evening after dinner. We had to stand up straight with a book balanced on our heads. If the book fell, we had to start the whole article over again. It was usually a heavy encyclopedia, too. The 'M' volume, I think, because it was the thickest. We had this beautiful set of Encyclopedia Britannica bound in dark green leather. My father bought it from a traveling salesman who came to the door in a torrential downpour. His suit was completely soaked through, and I think my father just felt sorry for him. He bought the whole set on an installment plan... \n\nOh, dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I apologize. But no, I haven't noticed myself speaking any slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you're just going through the motions with the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: How long? Her daughter came all the way up from Florida to help her recover, but honestly, the daughter was more of a hindrance than a help. She kept trying to rearrange Mrs. Higgins' kitchen cabinets while she was laid up in bed. Who does that? She moved all the good china to the bottom shelf where anyone could just bump into it. It was Royal Doulton, too, the Old Balloon Seller pattern, I believe. My grandmother had a few pieces of that same pattern, but she kept them in a beautiful locked mahogany curio cabinet in the formal dining room. The key was this tiny, delicate brass thing that she kept on a black velvet ribbon. She used to let me hold it when I was a very little girl, but I had to wash my hands with lye soap first so I wouldn't tarnish it...\n\nOh, goodness me, I've wandered completely off again, haven't I? No... no, I haven't really had any good days. It's just been this constant, heavy feeling for about a month now. Ever since she died so unexpectedly. \n\nIt was a Tuesday when it happened. I always remember because Tuesday is the day the street sweepers come through our neighborhood. They make such a dreadful racket, don't they? You have to move your car off the street by 8:00 AM or they give you a fifty-dollar ticket. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, got three tickets in one month last year because he kept forgetting. He’s getting quite forgetful these days, poor man. He used to be a high school history teacher. Taught European history, I think. Or maybe it was American? No, it must have been European, because he always talked about a trip he took to Prague in the seventies. He brought back this beautiful bohemian glass vase. It was a deep ruby red with gold etching all around the rim. He kept it right in his front window where the afternoon sun would catch it. It cast the most beautiful red shadows across his living room floor. He had hardwood floors, you know, the original oak from when the houses were built in the twenties. They don't make floors like that anymore, do they? Everything is that cheap laminate now that bubbles up if you spill a drop of water...\n\nOh, dear, I've done it again. We had this beautiful set of Encyclopedia Britannica bound in dark green leather. My father bought it from a traveling salesman who came to the door in a torrential downpour. His suit was completely soaked through, and I think my father just felt sorry for him. He bought the whole set on an installment plan... \n\nOh, dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? I apologize. But no, I haven't noticed myself speaking any slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you're just going through the motions with the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: How long? Well, I suppose it's been a few months now. I still make myself show up for things, you know, like my book club or the Thursday dance night. But lately, I just sit there feeling like I'm behind a pane of glass. Even my garden and cooking for the neighbors don't give me that little lift they used to. \n\nI used to love baking for Mr. and Mrs. Higgins next door. Poor Mrs. Higgins broke her hip back in the spring. Slipped right on her front walkway on a patch of wet leaves. They had to put three pins in it. Her daughter came all the way up from Florida to help her recover, but honestly, the daughter was more of a hindrance than a help. She kept trying to rearrange Mrs. Higgins' kitchen cabinets while she was laid up in bed. Who does that? She moved all the good china to the bottom shelf where anyone could just bump into it. It was Royal Doulton, too, the Old Balloon Seller pattern, I believe. My grandmother had a few pieces of that same pattern, but she kept them in a beautiful locked mahogany curio cabinet in the formal dining room. The key was this tiny, delicate brass thing that she kept on a black velvet ribbon. She used to let me hold it when I was a very little girl, but I had to wash my hands with lye soap first so I wouldn't tarnish it...\n\nOh, goodness me, I've wandered completely off again, haven't I? I am so sorry. But yes, it's been a few months now that I've just felt disconnected from it all, just going through the motions without any of the joy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of feeling somewhat better, even for a few days, since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Better? No... no, I haven't really had any good days. It's just been this constant, heavy feeling for about a month now. Ever since she died so unexpectedly. \n\nIt was a Tuesday when it happened. I always remember because Tuesday is the day the street sweepers come through our neighborhood. They make such a dreadful racket, don't they? You have to move your car off the street by 8:00 AM or they give you a fifty-dollar ticket. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, got three tickets in one month last year because he kept forgetting. He’s getting quite forgetful these days, poor man. He used to be a high school history teacher. Taught European history, I think. Or maybe it was American? No, it must have been European, because he always talked about a trip he took to Prague in the seventies. He brought back this beautiful bohemian glass vase. It was a deep ruby red with gold etching all around the rim. He kept it right in his front window where the afternoon sun would catch it. It cast the most beautiful red shadows across his living room floor. He had hardwood floors, you know, the original oak from when the houses were built in the twenties. They don't make floors like that anymore, do they? Everything is that cheap laminate now that bubbles up if you spill a drop of water...\n\nOh, dear, I've done it again. I'm so sorry, I just keep drifting off. What were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling better. No, it's just been a steady month of this awful fog since she passed."
